- metrics: likes=134, reposts=13, replies=3, quotes=0, bookmarks=18, impressions=0

The best companies demonstrate micro chaos and macro structure.

Micro chaos (resembling anarchy) allows the interesting ideas to survive, and ultimately the best ideas to win.

Macro structure offers predictability, accountability, and debuggability.

The reason you can do what people say is impossible is because the world is incredibly miscalibrated.

People wildly underestimate how feasible it is to do something new. At the same time, they underestimate the difficulty of doing something again.

Do not listen to other people.

Bill's ambition of vision early on:

"We did BASICs for everything and we knew from the beginning we weren’t the BASIC company. We were going to be a software company."

It is now common for companies to paint a large manifest destiny. But Microsoft was before it was obvious 4/

Referenced post text:
  - (replied_to) `1209164522964963335` Bill on competitive inspiration: /  / "Our competition said, 'Windows had the risk of just becoming a set of poorly debugged device drivers.' That’s a direct quote, which is great, when your competition motivates you so eloquently. And it was true. That was an actual risk." 3/

Spend the money on a nice office.

Be frugal on just about everything else, but get a nice office.

It's hard to appreciate just how much your office becomes a representation of your company. Plus, you're going to spend 10k+ hrs in it.

While this is common advice, it is the most important.

Surround yourself with the BEST people. Not only people who are smart and ambitious—those who reinforce your best qualities and call you out on your worst.

This is true for teammates, customers, investors, and friends.

Referenced post text:
  - (replied_to) `1334171700120862721` We announced some big @scale_AI news. The illusion with this sort of news is that it always looks like an overnight success. /  / I wanted to mention a few things I've learned along the way. I hope they'll be useful to other folks who are looking to build the future. /  / 👇Thread

### 2020-12-03 00:24:56 GMT+8 · reply
- ID: `1334171701844746240`  
- UTC: 2020-12-02T16:24:56.000Z  
- URL: https://x.com/alexandr_wang/status/1334171701844746240  
- lang: None  
- metrics: likes=37, reposts=2, replies=2, quotes=1, bookmarks=1, impressions=0
- referenced: replied_to:1334171701001678848

As you scale, you will be confronted by one fork over and over: do I try to please everyone, or do I do what I think is right?

It is SO much more important to be authentic than it is to please everyone.

It is OK to be idiosyncratic sometimes.

Authenticity &gt;&gt;&gt; anything else.

For most of the time building @scale_AI, tactics have been more important than strategy.

The tech community has a tendency to overweigh strategy because it is more narrative and easier to think about.

But, I've found that success often comes down to extreme tactical orientation

---

### 2020-12-03 00:24:56 GMT+8 · reply
- ID: `1334171703669211136`  
- UTC: 2020-12-02T16:24:56.000Z  
- URL: https://x.com/alexandr_wang/status/1334171703669211136  
- lang: None  
- metrics: likes=47, reposts=5, replies=2, quotes=1, bookmarks=6, impressions=0
- referenced: replied_to:1334171702696181760

If you are a builder, adopt a mindset of short-term pessimism but long-term optimism.

You need to see the problems clear-eyed today, but it is oftentimes totally unexpected what is possible in the long-term if you remain focused.

I've found that optimism often shapes reality.

Closing on simple advice, but so important:

Focus! There are strong non-linear benefits to extreme focus. Every great business was initially focused on something so narrow it didn't seem consequential.

Fight the temptation to be everything to everyone. It just doesn't work.

.@ouraring vs Apple Watch is a case study in focus

Oura focused on multi-day charge sleep/activity tracking. To me, it’s much better than the ⌚️

The ⌚️ does “more”, but worse for the core use case. Apple could have built a ring, but instead conformed to existing big markets(⌚️)

Idealists often think that the best ideas win—that Darwinian idea selection selects for the highest quality ideas.

Instead, it actually selects for the most viral and memetic (highest k-factor). So that means if your aim is to have the best ideas, you should often be contrarian.

The right motto for a technology company is:

"Move fast, shrink things"

- Shrink batch sizes (big visions happen in small steps!)
- Shrink team sizes (2-pizza teams are brilliant)
- Shrink products (easier to sell a calf than a herd)

More shrinking -&gt; less breaking

"I worry about the good years because we learn less about the business in the good years than the bad years."

Framing business as a vehicle for continuous learning is key to long-term success—winning comes from learning from your losses.

A simple formula for being great at anything:

1) Spend a LOT of time on it. Unreasonable amounts
2) Find a teacher who instills a love of excellence and a love of craft
3) Constantly seek the feedback of honest critics
4) Treat failure as a vehicle for learning
5) Do NOT give up

Operating is paranoia.

Despite all the fair criticisms, there’s nowhere else where it feels like the future of the world is being actively invented.

NYC/Miami/LA/Austin/London-heads, prove me wrong 🙃

### 2022-12-25 23:57:26 GMT+8 · original
- ID: `1607042844182851584`  
- UTC: 2022-12-25T15:57:26.000Z  
- URL: https://x.com/alexandr_wang/status/1607042844182851584  
- lang: en  
- metrics: likes=436, reposts=46, replies=16, quotes=2, bookmarks=133, impressions=77178

Heard someone say “I don’t want to waste brain space on learning Chinese”

PSA—that’s not how it works at all.

Consistently *retrieving* information both deepens connections with the rest of your knowledge and frees up resources &amp; working memory for more abstract thought.

🧵

---

### 2022-12-25 23:57:26 GMT+8 · reply
- ID: `1607042846363877376`  
- UTC: 2022-12-25T15:57:26.000Z  
- URL: https://x.com/alexandr_wang/status/1607042846363877376  
- lang: en  
- metrics: likes=55, reposts=5, replies=2, quotes=0, bookmarks=3, impressions=10203
- referenced: replied_to:1607042844182851584

Memorizing actually allows for new conceptual understanding, it’s not just rote BS.

And while there is some “wetware” limit based on the number of synapses, that limit is roughly the memory size of the movie of your entire life. It’s why some people can have photographic memory

  - (replied_to) `1607042844182851584` Heard someone say “I don’t want to waste brain space on learning Chinese” /  / PSA—that’s not how it works at all. /  / Consistently *retrieving* information both deepens connections with the rest of your knowledge and frees up resources &amp; working memory for more abstract thought. /  / 🧵

Knowledge compounds on itself, and enables you to perform more abstract thinking.

You're not going to 'run out of space', and deliberately trying to save space by not remembering is hampering your ability for higher order thought.

my interests roughly by age

0-12—math+chess all the time
13-17—math+algos+physics all the time
17-25—stopped doing math. tried to be cool. coded, read literature, played poker, traveled, went to concerts/parties
25+—math all the time

as I tell my nephew, math will set you free

one reason why math can be better for your brain than coding:

coding is constrained in instantiating the imminently possible, thereby limiting the scale &amp; abstraction of thought

math forces you to think about the limits of human thought (ex: cardinalities of infinity)

### 2026-11-09 14:24:57 GMT+8 · original
- ID: `2119677603862692029`  
- UTC: 2026-11-09T06:24:57.000Z  
- URL: https://x.com/alexandr_wang/status/2119677603862692029  
- lang: en  
- metrics: likes=4143, reposts=153, replies=154, quotes=45, bookmarks=1335, impressions=512106

i forgot about this until @ScottWu46 reminded me, but back in january 2017 when we were 19, scott and i had lunch in SF

at lunch, we talked about how our IMO/IOI/math competition gang could be the next paypal mafia. i knew the competition kids were geniuses by any standard, and felt confident in time we could take the tech world by storm

fast forward nearly 10 years later, and many of our friends from that time are some of the most exciting people in AI and technology (@ScottWu46, @akshat_b, @chameleon_jeff, Jerry Ma, @randomjohnnyh, @0xShitTrader, @stevenkplus1, @thejessezhang, @vickie_ye_, @alexwei_, @demi_guo_, @d_m_ziegler, @hughbzhang, @_mohansolo, @MarkSellke, and many others i'm sure i'm forgetting)

i'm now at that age where i reminisce about everyone i grew up with, and i've known most of these people since middle school (via MATHCOUNTS!). even though we were scattered around the country, we would gchat about math problems and hang out on AoPS, then we did math/algo camps together, and then we did internships together.

very crazy to think about the degree of sheer brilliance of this group when we were just teenagers, and so special to see so many go on to leave their mark on the world.

note to parents: if your kid is on gchat all day solving math problems with some anonymous group of cross-country asian kids, (s)he may just turn out alright.
